> AGENT

Short version: it is a decent internal editor architecture, but not yet a great “developer platform” component.

It has some good seams:

- `DataProvider` is a genuinely useful storage boundary: [DataProvider.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/data/DataProvider.ts:53). You can swap timeline loading/saving, asset registry, uploads, waveform/profile loading.
- The timeline store is split into data / ops / chrome / playback slices, which is directionally good: [timelineStore.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/hooks/timelineStore.ts:460).
- The persisted model is reasonably centralized around `TimelineConfig`, `TimelineClip`, `TrackDefinition`, `AssetRegistry`, and `ResolvedTimelineConfig`: [types/index.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/types/index.ts:82).
- Effects are probably the best extension story. Built-ins and dynamic effects go through registries/schema-ish params, so adding effect-like behavior is relatively clean.
- Rendering dispatch for sequences is fairly centralized in [TimelineRenderer.tsx](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/compositions/TimelineRenderer.tsx:174).

But I would not call it easy to build on top of yet. The biggest issue is that the practical integration API is still “mount the whole Reigh editor provider/shell,” not “compose editor primitives.” `VideoEditorShell` only exposes `mode`, `timelineId`, and `onCreateTimeline`: [VideoEditorShell.tsx](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/components/VideoEditorShell.tsx:70). The shell owns layout, timeline, preview, inspector, render controls, dialogs, responsive behavior, etc. That makes it good as an app feature, but awkward as a reusable editor surface.

The annoying parts for developers would be:

1. **Provider is too app-coupled.**  
   `VideoEditorProvider` wires router params, app agent chat, shots context, lightbox, selection store, pending “add generation” flow, custom effects, and timeline state together: [VideoEditorProvider.tsx](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/contexts/VideoEditorProvider.tsx:69). That means a developer can’t easily say “give me editor state and I’ll bring my own UI.”

2. **Higher-level components are context-bound.**  
   `PreviewPanel`, `PropertiesPanel`, `TimelineEditor`, and `CompactPreview` mostly pull from global editor context/selectors rather than accepting explicit props. Some leaf pieces are reusable, like `RemotionPreview`, `ParameterControls`, and maybe `TimelineCanvas`, but the useful assembled pieces are hard to recombine.

3. **The state API is very broad.**  
   `TimelineEditorOpsContextValue` exposes a huge bag of handlers: selection, clip editing, track management, dragging, assets, registry patching, overlay editing, upload, etc. [useTimelineState.types.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/hooks/useTimelineState.types.ts:92). That is powerful, but not ergonomic. A developer has to understand internal vocabulary before doing simple actions.

4. **Data canonicalization is not universal.**  
   UI load repairs/migrates data in [timeline-data.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/lib/timeline-data.ts:393), but the AI timeline agent appears to work closer to raw DB config. That creates a real risk that different writers understand the same timeline differently.

5. **Duration semantics are misaligned.**  
   UI/render helpers treat duration one way, agent tools fall back to asset registry duration in other cases, and some render paths compute sequence duration manually. That is exactly the kind of “why did this clip render differently than the editor showed?” problem that makes extension work painful.

6. **Sequence extensibility is halfway there.**  
   Runtime render lookup is registry-based, but metadata/validation exists in multiple places: frontend metadata and edge validation. New sequence param types also require UI and validator changes. So it is not a clean “drop in a new clip type” plugin model.

7. **Agent commands are not modular.**  
   Adding a command means touching schema prose, parser unions/parsers, registry mapping, handlers, and tests. That will slow down anyone trying to build automation on top of the editor.

My take: the editor is modular enough for the current team to keep evolving it, but not yet modular enough to invite other developers to confidently build new editor experiences, new clip types, or new automation without stepping on internals.

The highest-leverage fixes would be:

- Create a real public editor API/barrel: `VideoEditorRoot`, `useTimeline`, `useTimelineActions`, `TimelinePreview`, `TimelineCanvas`, `Inspector`, typed command helpers.
- Split `VideoEditorProvider` into a pure editor state provider and Reigh-specific integrations.
- Make canonicalization, duration calculation, serialization, and validation shared by UI, import, render, and agent tools.
- Turn clip/sequence/effect/agent-command registration into descriptor objects instead of parallel lists.
- Document one blessed way to mutate timelines: ideally a small command layer over `applyEdit`, not raw `rows/meta/config` surgery everywhere.

So: good bones, but not yet a clean component platform. The hard part is less React component decomposition and more making one canonical timeline domain model that every UI, renderer, importer, and agent command must go through.

Can you think through what needs to be done in more detail and break it down into kind of like two week sprints? And again, just think about how we maintain the functionality while making it into a really nice developer experience so that the developer can tweak the front ends that they can use the data in different ways that they can kind of like change how things process that they can change how things kind of, um, yeah. Worked in general across the.

> AGENT

Yes. I’d approach this as a staged extraction/refactor, not a rewrite. The goal is to keep today’s Reigh editor working while progressively creating a clean “developer surface” underneath it.

The north star: a developer should be able to do these without spelunking through internals:

- Mount the whole editor with custom storage/render hooks.
- Compose their own UI from timeline, preview, inspector, asset, and playback primitives.
- Read timeline data in one canonical format.
- Mutate timeline data through documented commands.
- Add clip types, effects, sequences, render routes, and agent commands through registration objects.
- Trust that UI, render, import, backend, and agent tools interpret the same data the same way.

**Sprint 1: Stabilize The Domain Model**
Goal: make timeline data predictable before exposing it.

- Define one canonical timeline domain module, probably under something like `src/tools/video-editor/domain`.
- Move duration helpers, canonicalization, repair, migration, serialization, and validation behind a small public API.
- Make UI load, save, import, render, and agent tooling use the same helpers.
- Fix known semantic mismatches:
  - duration calculation
  - `hold` vs `from/to`
  - `speed`
  - pinned shot group membership
  - asset registry lookup
- Add regression tests that compare UI-derived, render-derived, and agent-derived interpretations of the same timeline.

Deliverable: one blessed way to turn persisted timeline config into canonical runtime data, and one blessed way to serialize it back.

**Sprint 2: Create A Public Timeline API**
Goal: developers can read and mutate timeline state without understanding `rows/meta/clipOrder` internals.

- Add a public barrel, for example:
  - `VideoEditorRoot`
  - `useTimelineData`
  - `useTimelineSelection`
  - `useTimelinePlayback`
  - `useTimelineActions`
  - `useTimelineAssets`
  - `useTimelineRender`
- Hide low-level `rows`, `meta`, and `clipOrder` from the default API.
- Add documented command-style mutations:
  - `addClip`
  - `updateClip`
  - `moveClip`
  - `trimClip`
  - `splitClip`
  - `deleteClip`
  - `addTrack`
  - `moveTrack`
  - `registerAsset`
- Keep `applyEdit` available internally, but discourage external direct use.
- Add type tests or example tests showing that external callers can build common workflows.

Deliverable: a developer can build a custom toolbar or inspector using stable hooks instead of internal store selectors.

**Sprint 3: Split Core Provider From Reigh Integrations**
Goal: make the editor usable outside the full Reigh app shell.

- Split `VideoEditorProvider` into:
  - `TimelineEditorCoreProvider`
  - `ReighVideoEditorIntegrations`
- Core provider should require only:
  - `timelineId`
  - `userId`
  - `dataProvider`
  - optional registries/config
- Move app-specific behavior out:
  - router query params
  - media lightbox
  - shots context
  - project context
  - agent chat registration
  - pending generation-add flow
  - global selection store coupling where possible
- Preserve current behavior by wrapping core provider with Reigh integrations in `VideoEditorPage`.

Deliverable: existing editor still works, but a developer can mount the core editor in another route/test/demo without Reigh-specific contexts.

**Sprint 4: Make UI Components Composable**
Goal: developers can swap layout and build custom frontends.

- Convert major panels into prop-capable or provider-light components:
  - `TimelinePanel`
  - `PreviewPanel`
  - `InspectorPanel`
  - `AssetPanel`
  - `PlaybackControls`
  - `RenderControls`
- Keep current shell as `ReighVideoEditorShell`.
- Add lower-level primitives:
  - `TimelineCanvas`
  - `TimelineTrack`
  - `TimelineClip`
  - `RemotionPreview`
  - `OverlayEditor`
- Create one or two example custom layouts:
  - compact editor
  - preview-first editor
  - inspector-only sidebar
- Export prop types for reusable components.

Deliverable: frontend customization is real. Developers can keep the same timeline engine while replacing layout, panels, controls, or inspector.

**Sprint 5: Formalize Extension Registries**
Goal: new behavior is added through descriptors, not scattered edits.

- Create descriptor types for:
  - clip types
  - effects
  - sequences
  - property editors
  - render routes
  - asset handlers
  - agent commands
- Unify duplicated built-in clip type lists.
- Make clip type registration include:
  - render component
  - timeline display component or metadata
  - inspector component
  - default clip factory
  - validation schema
  - supported render route
- Move sequence metadata and validation into shared descriptors instead of frontend/edge duplication.
- Add tests that register a fake clip type and verify it appears in editor, renderer, validation, and serialization.

Deliverable: adding a new clip/sequence/effect is a predictable checklist, ideally one descriptor plus tests.

**Sprint 6: Fix Render And Processing Customization**
Goal: developers can change how timelines process/render without patching editor internals.

- Finish worker render routing from UI. Right now route detection exists but the UI path does not fully enqueue worker renders.
- Define render pipeline interfaces:
  - browser render
  - worker render
  - external render provider
  - preview-only placeholder
- Let clip/sequence descriptors declare render capabilities.
- Add hooks/events:
  - before render
  - after render
  - render failed
  - asset materialization
  - missing capability fallback
- Make generated/worker-only clips show clear preview behavior while still renderable via the correct backend path.

Deliverable: a developer can add a processing/render backend without rewriting `useRenderState`.

**Sprint 7: Modularize Agent Commands And Automation**
Goal: automation becomes a first-class extension surface.

- Replace parallel command definitions with command descriptors:
  - name
  - schema
  - parser
  - handler
  - help text
  - examples
  - permission/safety metadata
- Make agent commands call the same canonical timeline command layer from Sprint 2.
- Ensure agent operations maintain pinned groups, durations, asset registry, and validation.
- Add first-class commands for sequence/themed clip creation, not just param patching.
- Add snapshot tests for command schemas and behavior.

Deliverable: adding an agent command no longer means touching parser, prose schema, registry, and handler separately.

**Sprint 8: Developer Experience Polish**
Goal: make it feel like a small internal SDK, not just refactored app code.

- Add docs:
  - “Mount the full editor”
  - “Build a custom editor UI”
  - “Read timeline data”
  - “Mutate timeline data”
  - “Add a clip type”
  - “Add an effect”
  - “Add a render provider”
  - “Add an agent command”
- Add Storybook or local demo routes for primitives.
- Add fixture timelines for common cases.
- Add package-style exports, even if still internal.
- Add “compatibility tests” ensuring old Reigh editor behavior still works.
- Mark internal APIs clearly.

Deliverable: a new developer can build something useful in an afternoon without reading the whole video editor folder.

**How To Maintain Functionality During This**
The critical rule is adapter-first refactoring.

Don’t replace the current editor. Put new APIs under the current editor, then migrate one consumer at a time.

Good sequence:

1. Preserve current `VideoEditorPage` and `VideoEditorShell`.
2. Extract canonical domain helpers and point current code at them.
3. Add new public hooks that wrap existing store/selectors.
4. Split provider, but keep old provider as compatibility wrapper.
5. Make panels composable, but keep old shell layout.
6. Introduce registries while registering today’s built-ins as default descriptors.
7. Move agent/render/import paths onto the same domain APIs.

That gives you a continuously working editor while turning it into a platform.

**Priority If You Only Do Three Sprints**
I’d do these first:

1. Canonical domain model.
2. Public timeline API.
3. Core provider split.

Those are the foundation. Without them, component extraction is mostly cosmetic, because developers still hit confusing data semantics and app-coupled state. Once those three are solid, the frontend customization and extension system become much easier.

> AGENT

Yes, that plan is pointed at the right outcome. It would make the editor much easier for both human developers and agents to change, because it moves from “understand the whole app and mutate internals carefully” to “use typed ports, commands, slots, and registries.”

The main thing I’d add: **agent-editability should be treated as a first-class acceptance criterion**, not just an outcome of better architecture.

Right now the plan covers most of it, but I’d add or sharpen these pieces:

**1. Command Bus Should Move Earlier**
Sprint 5 is valuable, but for agent editing it may be too late. Agents need a safe, documented mutation layer before they can reliably change timelines.

I’d either pull a minimal command bus into Sprint 1/2 or add a “command facade” early:

```ts
timeline.commands.addClip(...)
timeline.commands.updateClip(...)
timeline.commands.splitClip(...)
timeline.commands.deleteClip(...)
timeline.commands.registerAsset(...)
timeline.commands.setClipParams(...)
```

Even if undo/redo comes later, the important part is that agents stop writing `rows`, `meta`, `clipOrder`, and `config` directly.

**2. Add A Machine-Readable Capability Manifest**
For agents and plugin authors, documentation is useful but not enough. The editor should expose a structured manifest:

```ts
{
  clipTypes: [...],
  commands: [...],
  panels: [...],
  renderTargets: [...],
  assetResolvers: [...],
  schemas: {...}
}
```

Then an agent can ask: “What clip types exist? What params does `title-card` need? What commands are safe? Which render routes support this clip?”

This belongs around Sprint 4/5.

**3. Add Validation And Dry-Run Everywhere**
For agent workflows, every command should support:

```ts
validate(command)
dryRun(command)
apply(command)
```

That lets an agent propose edits, check if they are legal, preview the resulting state, and only then apply.

Acceptance criterion: an agent can generate a batch of timeline edits and get structured errors like:

```ts
{
  ok: false,
  error: "clip_duration_invalid",
  path: "clips[3].to",
  suggestion: "to must be greater than from"
}
```

**4. Add A Stable JSON Patch / Transaction Format**
If an agent is editing remotely or through an API, it needs a portable edit format, not React hooks.

Something like:

```ts
{
  transactionId: "txn_123",
  commands: [
    { type: "clip.add", payload: {...} },
    { type: "clip.updateParams", clipId: "clip-1", patch: {...} }
  ]
}
```

This is also useful for undo, audit logs, collaborative editing, and reproducible bug reports.

**5. Make Plugin APIs Schema-First**
Your `defineClipType` idea is right. I’d require each plugin to provide schemas for:

- persisted clip params
- editor defaults
- inspector UI fields
- render capabilities
- command payloads

That makes it easy for a frontend developer, backend process, or agent to use the same extension without reverse engineering TypeScript components.

**6. Add A Headless Test Harness**
The embed demo is good, but I’d add an automated headless harness earlier.

Example acceptance test:

```ts
const editor = createHeadlessTimelineEditor({ dataProvider, registries });
await editor.commands.addClip(...);
await editor.commands.splitClip(...);
expect(editor.snapshot()).toMatchTimelineFixture();
```

This is what makes the system agent-friendly. An agent can make changes and run deterministic tests without needing to mount the whole UI.

**7. Add Event Hooks, Not Just Render Hooks**
For “change how things work in general,” developers need lifecycle events across the whole editor:

- `beforeCommand`
- `afterCommand`
- `onValidationError`
- `onSelectionChange`
- `onAssetRegistered`
- `onRenderQueued`
- `onTimelineSaved`
- `onConflict`
- `onExternalMutation`

This gives plugin authors ways to alter behavior without forking core.

**8. Separate UI Slots From Domain Slots**
Sprint 7 covers UI slots. I’d distinguish:

- UI slots: toolbar, inspector, panel, status bar
- domain slots: asset resolver, command middleware, render provider, clip type, validation policy

The second category is what lets developers “change how it works,” not just how it looks.

So my adjusted sprint emphasis would be:

- Sprint 1: Canonical model plus canonical validation/errors.
- Sprint 2: Ports/adapters plus early command facade.
- Sprint 3: Headless core plus headless test harness.
- Sprint 4: Clip-type registry plus schema/capability manifest.
- Sprint 5: Full command bus, transactions, undo/redo, dry-run.
- Sprint 6: Asset/render pipelines.
- Sprint 7: UI slots/panel registry/theme.
- Sprint 8: API freeze/docs/embed demo.

Would this make it “really easy”? After Sprint 4, it should be meaningfully easier. After Sprint 5, agents should be able to edit safely. After Sprint 8, it should feel like an internal SDK rather than a refactored app feature.

The biggest missing acceptance test I’d add is:

> A fresh agent, given only the public docs and manifest, can add a custom clip type, create a timeline using it, modify it through commands, render or preview it, and pass validation without importing internal files.

If that passes, the architecture is genuinely developer-friendly.

> AGENT

Yes. The clean way is: use **one cloud megaplan run per sprint**, with an **all-codex profile checked into the project**, and make each sprint idea file explicit enough that the cloud runner can operate autonomously.

One wrinkle I found locally: your `megaplan` launcher is currently broken here because the installed script points at a Python env where `megaplan` is not importable. The user config does show all phases already routed to `codex`, but before deploying cloud runs we’d need to repair the local launcher enough to run `megaplan cloud init/deploy`.

**Recommended Setup**

1. Add a project-local all-codex profile so cloud does not depend on your machine config:

```toml
# .megaplan/profiles.toml
[profiles.all-codex]
prep                  = "codex"
plan                  = "codex"
critique              = "codex"
gate                  = "codex"
revise                = "codex"
finalize              = "codex"
execute               = "codex"
review                = "codex"
loop_plan             = "codex"
loop_execute          = "codex"
tiebreaker_researcher = "codex"
tiebreaker_challenger = "codex"
```

2. Create sprint idea files, for example:

```text
.megaplan/ideas/video-editor-dx-sprint-01.md
.megaplan/ideas/video-editor-dx-sprint-02.md
...
```

Each one should include:
- scope
- hard non-regression requirements
- exact success criteria
- files/areas likely involved
- explicit “do not rewrite the editor”
- tests expected
- public API/developer-experience acceptance criteria

3. For Sprint 1, run cloud in autonomous mode with the all-codex profile:

```bash
megaplan cloud init
# edit cloud.yaml: provider railway, mode auto, profile all-codex, robustness robust/superrobust
megaplan cloud deploy
megaplan cloud attach
```

Inside the cloud runner, the plan should effectively do:

```bash
megaplan init \
  --project-dir /workspace/reigh-app \
  --profile all-codex \
  --robustness robust \
  --auto-approve \
  "$(cat .megaplan/ideas/video-editor-dx-sprint-01.md)"

megaplan auto --plan <plan-name>
```

Exact cloud command wiring depends on the scaffolded `cloud.yaml`, but that’s the shape.

**How I’d Run The Sprints**

I would not fire all eight at once. These are serial because Sprints 1, 4, and 5 all reshape core timeline/store architecture.

Run sequence:

1. `sprint-01-canonical-data-model`
2. review/merge
3. `sprint-02-ports-adapters-command-facade`
4. review/merge
5. `sprint-03-headless-core`
6. continue

Use `--from-doc` after Sprint 1 if we create a settled architecture doc:

```bash
megaplan init \
  --project-dir /workspace/reigh-app \
  --profile all-codex \
  --robustness robust \
  --auto-approve \
  --from-doc docs/design/video-editor-developer-platform.md \
  "$(cat .megaplan/ideas/video-editor-dx-sprint-02.md)"
```

**Cloud Operating Model**

For each cloud sprint:

```bash
megaplan cloud status
megaplan cloud logs
megaplan cloud attach
megaplan cloud exec -- megaplan status --plan <plan>
megaplan cloud exec -- git status --short
megaplan cloud exec -- pnpm test ...
```

At the end:

```bash
megaplan cloud exec -- git diff --stat
megaplan cloud exec -- git status --short
```

Then either have the cloud runner push a branch/PR, or pull the patch back locally and open the PR from here.

**Acceptance Criterion For Agent-Editability**

Every cloud sprint idea file should include this recurring must-have:

> All new developer-facing behavior must be accessible through documented public APIs, command objects, ports, registries, or typed manifests. Agents must not need to import internal hooks, mutate `rows/meta/clipOrder` directly, or depend on Reigh app contexts.

That keeps Megaplan from producing a good refactor that still isn’t agent-friendly.

**Immediate Next Step**

Before launching cloud, fix/verify the launcher:

```bash
megaplan config show
megaplan config profiles list
megaplan cloud --help
```

Right now those fail locally due the broken Python install path, so cloud orchestration can’t start from this shell yet. Once that’s repaired, we can scaffold `.megaplan/profiles.toml`, write Sprint 1’s idea file, run `megaplan cloud init`, and deploy the first all-codex run.

```json
{
```
```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: .megaplan/video-editor-dx-sprint-01-idea.md\n+Video editor developer platform Sprint 1: canonical timeline domain model.\n+\n+Context:\n+The Reigh video editor under src/tools/video-editor has useful primitives, but it is not yet easy for developers or agents to build on top of. Current pain points include multiple timeline shapes (TimelineClip, ClipMeta, ResolvedTimelineClip, TimelineData rows/meta/clipOrder), inconsistent duration semantics across UI/render/agent paths, Reigh-specific dependencies mixed into editor provider/components, and mutation paths that require internal knowledge.\n+\n+This sprint is the first two-week slice. It must preserve the current Reigh app behavior while establishing the domain foundation for later ports/adapters, headless core, clip-type plugins, command bus, render pipeline hooks, and UI slots.\n+\n+Goal:\n+Create a canonical timeline domain layer that all current editor paths can use without changing user-visible functionality.\n+\n+Primary outcomes:\n+- Establish one documented canonical interpretation of persisted TimelineConfig + AssetRegistry.\n+- Centralize repair/migration/canonicalization, duration calculation, validation, serialization, and structured errors.\n+- Remove or reduce divergent duration/shape logic between UI, Remotion renderer, import, and ai-timeline-agent paths.\n+- Prepare for agent-safe editing by defining validation/dry-run-friendly primitives, even if the full command bus ships in a later sprint.\n+\n+Important constraints:\n+- Do not rewrite the video editor.\n+- Do not change visible editor behavior except to fix clear inconsistencies.\n+- Keep the existing Reigh editor page and shell working.\n+- Keep current tests passing; add targeted tests for canonical domain behavior.\n+- Avoid broad UI refactors in this sprint unless required to route through the domain layer.\n+- Do not make plugin registries or UI slots yet; only shape the domain layer so those later sprints are easier.\n+\n+Areas to inspect:\n+- src/tools/video-editor/types/index.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/lib/timeline-data.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/lib/config-utils.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/lib/serialize.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/lib/migrate.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/hooks/useTimelineCommit.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/hooks/useTimelinePersistence.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/hooks/usePollSync.ts\n+- src/tools/video-editor/compositions/TimelineRenderer.tsx\n+- src/tools/video-editor/hooks/useDerivedTimeline.ts\n+- supabase/functions/timeline-import\n+- supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent\n+\n+Known issues to address or explicitly plan:\n+- UI load repairs/migrates before use, but ai-timeline-agent reads raw DB config.\n+- UI duration helpers and agent timeline tools can interpret clips with missing from/to differently.\n+- Some render paths compute sequence duration manually instead of using shared duration helpers.\n+- Import validation and editor serialization validation may accept/reject different shapes.\n+- Poll sync may advance configVersionRef before accepting polled data, potentially weakening conflict detection.\n+- Pinned shot group metadata is not maintained consistently by backend clip CRUD.\n+\n+Success criteria:\n+- There is a canonical domain module/API for timeline normalization, duration, validation, and serialization.\n+- UI, renderer, import, and agent paths either use this API or have explicit follow-up tasks for remaining migration.\n+- Tests cover equivalent duration/canonicalization behavior across at least UI/domain and agent/import-adjacent helpers.\n+- Current editor functionality remains intact.\n+- The resulting API is understandable for a later command bus and agent edit layer.\n+\n+Deliverable:\n+A code diff plus a concise notes section explaining the canonical domain boundary, remaining non-canonical call sites, and recommended Sprint 2 work.\n*** End Patch\n"
```

I fixed what I could and got the local all-Codex Megaplan chain running, but cloud launch is still blocked by the Megaplan repo access issue.

What I changed:

- Repaired `~/.local/bin/megaplan` so `megaplan config show` works again via `uvx --from megaplan-harness megaplan`.
- Added project all-Codex profile: [.megaplan/profiles.toml](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/.megaplan/profiles.toml).
- Added chained sprint idea files for Sprints 1-8 under `.megaplan/`.
- Added a chain/friction runbook: [.megaplan/video-editor-dx-chain-runbook.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/.megaplan/video-editor-dx-chain-runbook.md).
- Launched Sprint 1 as `video-editor-dx-sprint-01` and ran the legacy all-Codex loop through `clarify -> plan -> critique -> evaluate -> integrate -> critique -> evaluate -> integrate -> critique -> evaluate`.

Current Sprint 1 state:

- Plan: `video-editor-dx-sprint-01`
- State: `evaluated`
- Result: Megaplan escalated after iteration 3. I did **not** force execution.
- Main artifacts are in [.megaplan/plans/video-editor-dx-sprint-01](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/.megaplan/plans/video-editor-dx-sprint-01).

Why I stopped: critique found that `plan_v3.md` still risks changing visible UI semantics around clips with registry duration but missing `to`, and it broadened agent-command behavior beyond Sprint 1. I added a plan note saying not to execute it as-is.

Cloud blocker:

- `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan` exists but this Codex process cannot list/read it: `Operation not permitted`.
- The fallback package from `uvx` is older and has no `megaplan cloud`, no profiles command, and the older workflow only.
- So I could not launch the actual cloud machine. To unblock that, the Megaplan repo needs to be readable by this process, likely by granting terminal/Codex Full Disk Access, moving/cloning Megaplan into an accessible path like `Documents/reigh-workspace/megaplan`, or installing the cloud-capable version non-editably.

Useful friction found:

- Editable Megaplan install pointed at an inaccessible repo, breaking the launcher.
- Public/fallback CLI lacks cloud.
- Legacy Codex `clarify` and `plan` share a persistent session; resumed `plan` loses `--output-schema` and writes markdown instead of JSON. Workaround: `megaplan plan --agent codex --fresh`.
- Legacy CLI has no streaming progress for long phases.
- Legacy escalation has no targeted `replan`; only add-note, force-proceed, abort.

I also cleaned up generated schema noise from the old CLI, leaving only the intended project change visible: `.megaplan/profiles.toml`.

The hang was in `upload_file`: Railway’s noninteractive SSH path does not reliably consume piped stdin. I killed the stuck local process and am changing uploads to send a self-contained remote shell command with a base64 heredoc, including remote directory creation.

> TOOL

tool_use write_stdin

- `01a8461` fixed noninteractive Railway SSH usage.

- Focused Megaplan tests pass: cloud Railway, cloud chain wrapper/status, and chain tests.
- Worker is upgraded to `megaplan-harness 0.20.0`.
- Worker routing is now all Codex for plan/prep/critique/revise/gate/finalize/execute/review/loop/tiebreaker phases.
- `megaplan cloud exec` works against the worker through the cloud wrapper.
- `megaplan cloud chain` successfully uploaded the eight video-editor sprint ideas and started the remote chain.

Yes, it is working now.

Current live status: the cloud chain is running on the worker, Sprint 1 has moved from `initialized` to `planned`, and it is now actively running `critique via codex`. The chain status still shows Sprint 1 in progress and Sprints 2-8 pending, which is expected.

On “easy to understand”: much better, but not perfect yet.

What is now understandable:
- One command path exists: `megaplan cloud chain ...`.
- The chain runs in the cloud worker through Megaplan itself, not separate scripts.
- Status works through `megaplan cloud status --chain`.
- The cloud spec is explicit about Railway project/environment/service.
- The worker is pinned to a known Megaplan revision.
- The chain survives your local terminal because it runs in remote tmux.

What still felt annoying while setting it up:
- The old deployed worker entrypoint was stale and did not auto-upgrade Megaplan; source is fixed, but a future redeploy should pick that up.
- Railway SSH behavior is subtle: interactive `--session` works for attach, but breaks noninteractive commands.
- File upload via SSH stdin hung; I fixed this with self-contained base64 heredoc uploads.
- The chain status is good, but logs are still a bit indirect. You have to know about `/workspace/reigh-app/.megaplan/cloud-chain.log` or use `cloud exec`.

So: the functionality is working, and the main cloud path is coherent now. I’d call the DX “usable and understandable for us”, but it still needs a short doc/README and maybe a `megaplan cloud tail-chain` convenience command to be really obvious for someone new.

> DEVELOPER

so what would you recommend for us to make it downright elegant?

> AGENT

To make it elegant, I’d focus less on more orchestration features and more on making the happy path impossible to misunderstand.

1. **One Command For The Whole Flow**

Add:

```bash
megaplan cloud run-chain .megaplan/video-editor-dx-chain.yaml
```

That should:
- load `cloud.yaml`
- verify Railway auth/project/service
- verify worker Megaplan version
- upload ideas/spec
- start or resume `megaplan-chain`
- print status and exact follow-up commands

No separate mental model for upload, tmux, status markers, or remote paths.

2. **First-Class Chain Logs**

Add:

```bash
megaplan cloud logs --chain
megaplan cloud tail --chain
```

Today the real log exists, but you have to know it is at:

```text
/workspace/reigh-app/.megaplan/cloud-chain.log
```

That should be hidden behind a command.

3. **Built-In Doctor**

Add:

```bash
megaplan cloud doctor
```

It should check:
- local Railway CLI auth
- cloud spec validity
- project/environment/service resolve correctly
- remote worker reachable
- remote `megaplan` version/ref
- Codex auth exists
- git clone exists and branch is clean enough
- tmux available
- volume writable
- chain session running/stale/missing

This would have caught nearly every friction point I hit.

4. **Make Worker Version Drift Impossible**

The worker should always boot with the requested `megaplan.ref`, and `cloud status` should show:

```text
local ref: d7601e4
remote ref: d7601e4
package: 0.20.0
entrypoint ref: d7601e4
```

If remote is stale, print:

```bash
megaplan cloud upgrade
```

5. **Better Status Output**

Current JSON is useful but too raw. The default should be human-first:

```text
Cloud chain: running
Session: megaplan-chain
Milestone: 1/8 sprint-01-canonical-data-model
Plan: video-editor-developer-20260502-1729
Phase: critique via codex, healthy, 1m13s
Next check: ~2m
Logs: megaplan cloud logs --chain
Attach: megaplan cloud attach --session megaplan-chain
```

Keep `--json` for automation.

6. **No Remote Path Thinking**

The user should never need to know `/workspace/reigh-app/chain.yaml`. The local spec should use local paths, and Megaplan should map them to remote paths internally.

7. **Cloud Spec Scaffolding For Existing Workers**

Add:

```bash
megaplan cloud adopt railway
```

It should inspect the current Railway project/service and write a correct `cloud.yaml`. That would make “use this existing worker” smooth instead of manual.

8. **Stale Session Handling**

If `megaplan-chain` already exists, the CLI should inspect it:
- running healthy: report and do nothing
- dead/completed: archive and restart
- stale lock: explain exact recovery command
- wrong chain spec: ask for explicit `--replace`

Right now it just says session exists.

My recommended next sprint for Megaplan would be:

**Sprint A: Elegant Cloud Control Plane**
- `cloud doctor`
- `cloud run-chain`
- `cloud logs --chain`
- better human status
- remote version/ref reporting
- stale session handling

That would convert this from “works if you know the internals” to “feels like a real product interface.”

> DEVELOPER

Should there some kind of queue idea also that makes some items dependant on others and executed when it's done? Are different items being run in worktrees now? (like the bakeoff, so we can run multiple) and do we have a merge path?

> AGENT

I’m going to verify the current chain implementation rather than infer from the behavior we saw. The important questions are dependency graph, worktree isolation, parallelism, and merge semantics.

> AGENT

Yes, I think Megaplan should have a real queue/DAG concept. Right now `chain` is an ordered serial list, not a dependency-aware scheduler.

Current state:
- `chain.yaml` milestones run strictly in order.
- `branch:` exists in the spec, but the code says it is “currently informational.”
- Chain does not create worktrees.
- Chain does not run multiple milestones in parallel.
- Chain does not have a first-class merge path.
- Bakeoff does use separate worktrees and has a pick/merge flow, but that machinery is not used by chain.

What I’d recommend:

**1. Promote Chain To A Queue/DAG**
Let milestones declare dependencies:

```yaml
items:
  - id: canonical-data
    idea: ideas/sprint-01.md

  - id: ports-adapters
    idea: ideas/sprint-02.md
    depends_on: [canonical-data]

  - id: docs-demo
    idea: ideas/sprint-08.md
    depends_on: [clip-registry, ui-slots]
```

Then Megaplan can run any item whose dependencies are done.

**2. Add Worktree Isolation**
Each queue item should run in its own worktree:

```text
.megaplan-worktrees/chains/video-editor-dx/canonical-data
.megaplan-worktrees/chains/video-editor-dx/ports-adapters
```

This makes parallel work safe and gives each agent a clean branch.

**3. Add Merge Strategy**
For each item:

```yaml
merge:
  target: main
  strategy: pr
```

Supported strategies:
- `none`: produce patch only
- `fast_forward`: local merge if clean
- `pr`: push branch and open PR
- `stacked`: branch from dependency output branch

For this video-editor refactor, I’d prefer stacked or serial PRs for Sprints 1-4, because they touch overlapping core/store code.

**4. Add Dependency-Aware Parallelism**
Example:

```yaml
scheduler:
  max_parallel: 3
  conflict_policy: path_prefix
```

Then declare write scopes:

```yaml
items:
  - id: clip-registry
    owns:
      - src/tools/video-editor/clips/**
      - src/tools/video-editor/compositions/**

  - id: ui-slots
    owns:
      - src/tools/video-editor/components/**
```

Megaplan should only parallelize items whose dependencies and write scopes do not conflict.

**5. Add Queue Status**
The status should look like:

```text
Queue: video-editor-dx
Running: 2
Ready: 1
Blocked: 5

[done] canonical-data
[running] ports-adapters
[running] clip-registry
[ready] docs-demo
[blocked] command-bus depends_on: ports-adapters
```

**6. Reuse Bakeoff Internals**
Don’t build a second worktree system. Bakeoff already has:
- worktree creation
- status tracking
- patch comparison/merge primitives

Chain/queue should reuse that infrastructure, with different semantics:
- Bakeoff = multiple alternatives, choose one winner.
- Queue = multiple tasks, merge all successful outputs according to dependency order.

So my answer is: yes, there should be a queue/DAG. No, the current chain is not using worktrees or parallel merge. The elegant version is a dependency-aware queue where every item runs in an isolated worktree, ready items can run in parallel, and completed items have a defined merge/PR path.

> DEVELOPER

And can chaining be a first-class citizen?

> AGENT

Yes. It should be.

Right now chaining feels like a useful automation layer bolted onto Megaplan. To make it first-class, I’d promote it from “run this YAML list” into a core Megaplan object with its own lifecycle, state, commands, UI-shaped status, logs, and recovery model.

What first-class should mean:

1. **A Chain Is A Named Entity**

```bash
megaplan chain init video-editor-dx --spec .megaplan/video-editor-dx.yaml
megaplan chain start video-editor-dx
megaplan chain status video-editor-dx
megaplan chain logs video-editor-dx
megaplan chain pause video-editor-dx
megaplan chain resume video-editor-dx
megaplan chain cancel video-editor-dx
```

No more “where is the spec file?” as the main identity.

2. **Cloud Uses The Same Chain Object**

```bash
megaplan cloud chain start video-editor-dx
megaplan cloud chain status video-editor-dx
megaplan cloud chain logs video-editor-dx
```

Local and cloud should differ only by execution backend. The object model should be the same.

3. **State Lives Under `.megaplan/chains/<name>`**

```text
.megaplan/chains/video-editor-dx/
  chain.yaml
  state.json
  events.jsonl
  items/
    sprint-01/
      plan_ref.json
      logs/
      result.json
```

That makes chains inspectable, resumable, and agent-friendly.

4. **Queue/DAG Instead Of Just Ordered List**

Keep ordered lists as the simple case, but support:

```yaml
items:
  - id: sprint-01
  - id: sprint-02
    depends_on: [sprint-01]
  - id: docs
    depends_on: [sprint-04, sprint-07]
```

A chain becomes a queue of blocked, ready, running, done, failed items.

5. **Worktree Execution Built In**

Each item should have an execution workspace:

```yaml
execution:
  isolation: worktree
  max_parallel: 3
```

This makes parallelism real and safe.

6. **Merge Is Part Of The Lifecycle**

Each item should end in one of:

```text
done_unmerged
merged
pr_opened
failed
blocked
```

Commands:

```bash
megaplan chain merge video-editor-dx sprint-01
megaplan chain open-prs video-editor-dx
megaplan chain land video-editor-dx --ready
```

7. **Events, Not Just Status**

Chains need an append-only event log:

```text
item_ready
item_started
plan_created
phase_completed
item_failed
item_merged
dependency_unblocked
```

That gives good status, recovery, and debugging.

8. **Human-Friendly Defaults**

This should work:

```bash
megaplan chain start .megaplan/video-editor-dx.yaml --cloud
```

And print:

```text
Started chain video-editor-dx in cloud.

Running:
  sprint-01 canonical-data-model: plan via codex

Next:
  sprint-02 blocked on sprint-01

Watch:
  megaplan cloud chain status video-editor-dx
  megaplan cloud chain logs video-editor-dx
```

My recommendation: build this as **Megaplan Chains v2**. Keep current `chain start --spec` as compatibility, but internally migrate it into a named chain object. That gives you an elegant product surface without breaking the working path we just fixed.

> DEVELOPER

Think through the right abstracion

> AGENT

The right abstraction is probably not “chain” as the primitive. The right primitive is a **Run Graph**.

A chain is just the simplest run graph: every item depends on the previous item.

**Core Abstraction: Run Graph**

A Run Graph is a durable orchestration object containing:

```yaml
id: video-editor-dx
mode: code

items:
  - id: canonical-data-model
    kind: megaplan
    idea: ideas/sprint-01.md
    depends_on: []
    isolation: worktree
    merge: pr

  - id: ports-adapters
    kind: megaplan
    idea: ideas/sprint-02.md
    depends_on: [canonical-data-model]
    isolation: worktree
    merge: stacked-pr

scheduler:
  max_parallel: 3
  conflict_policy: write_scope

execution:
  backend: cloud
```

Then Megaplan can offer friendly names:

- `chain` = ordered run graph
- `queue` = dependency-aware run graph
- `bakeoff` = competitive run graph where only one branch wins
- `batch` = independent run graph with no dependencies

But internally they are all the same orchestration model.

**Why This Is Better Than “Chain”**
“Chain” implies serial execution. That is too narrow.

You want:
- serial sprints
- parallel independent tasks
- dependencies
- retry/replan per item
- merge gates
- PR creation
- maybe bakeoff-style alternatives
- cloud persistence

Those are all graph concerns, not chain concerns.

**The Object Model**

Use four layers:

1. **Graph**
The whole orchestration.

```text
video-editor-dx
```

Owns state, config, event log, scheduler, backend.

2. **Item**
A unit of work.

```text
sprint-01-canonical-data
```

Usually maps to one Megaplan plan, but could also be a shell command, test suite, doc generation, deploy, review, or merge.

3. **Attempt**
A concrete execution of an item.

```text
attempt-001
attempt-002-after-rework
```

Attempts matter because retries should not overwrite history.

4. **Artifact**
The output of an attempt.

```text
plan name
branch
patch
PR URL
test result
review verdict
merge commit
```

**State Machine**

Graph item states should be explicit:

```text
pending
blocked
ready
running
needs_review
done_unmerged
merged
failed
skipped
cancelled
```

This is more useful than just `done` / `failed`.

Example:

```text
done_unmerged
```

means “the agent finished, but the work is not integrated.”

That distinction is essential.

**Dependencies**

Dependencies should be typed:

```yaml
depends_on:
  - item: canonical-data-model
    requires: merged
```

Possible requirements:

```text
done
approved
done_unmerged
merged
pr_opened
artifact:docs/api.md
```

This lets you say:

- Sprint 2 can start after Sprint 1 is planned.
- Sprint 3 can start only after Sprint 2 is merged.
- Docs can start once APIs exist, even before implementation is merged.

**Isolation**

Isolation should be per item:

```yaml
isolation:
  type: worktree
  base: main
```

Or:

```yaml
isolation:
  type: stacked-worktree
  base_item: canonical-data-model
```

This matters because some tasks can run from `main`, while others need the output of a dependency.

**Merge Policy**

Merge should be first-class, not an afterthought.

```yaml
merge:
  mode: pr
  target: main
  require_review: true
  after: tests_pass
```

Useful modes:

```text
none
patch
local_merge
pr
stacked_pr
auto_merge
```

For your video-editor work, I’d use:

- Sprints 1-4: serial or stacked PRs
- Sprints 5-8: partly parallel once core types and ports are stable

**Scheduler**

The scheduler should answer one question:

> What can safely run now?

Inputs:
- dependency states
- write scopes
- max parallelism
- merge policy
- available workers
- backend health

Example:

```yaml
scheduler:
  max_parallel: 3
  conflict_policy: write_scope
```

Each item can declare:

```yaml
writes:
  - src/tools/video-editor/types/**
  - src/tools/video-editor/lib/**
```

If two ready items overlap, Megaplan should serialize them.

**Cloud Backend**

Cloud should not have a separate mental model. It should just be a backend:

```yaml
execution:
  backend: cloud
  provider: railway
```

Then:

```bash
megaplan graph start video-editor-dx
megaplan graph status video-editor-dx
```

and:

```bash
megaplan cloud graph status video-editor-dx
```

are views over the same object.

**My Recommended Naming**

User-facing commands:

```bash
megaplan run init video-editor-dx --from chain.yaml
megaplan run start video-editor-dx --cloud
megaplan run status video-editor-dx
megaplan run logs video-editor-dx
megaplan run pause video-editor-dx
megaplan run resume video-editor-dx
megaplan run merge-ready video-editor-dx
```

Keep aliases:

```bash
megaplan chain start ...
megaplan queue start ...
megaplan bakeoff run ...
```

But internally they compile to `RunGraph`.

**The Key Design Principle**

Do not make “plan” the top-level durable object for multi-step work.

A plan is one item’s reasoning/execution lifecycle.

The durable top-level object should be:

```text
RunGraph -> Item -> Attempt -> Plan/Artifact
```

That gives you chaining, queues, dependencies, parallelism, retries, worktrees, PRs, and cloud execution without inventing separate systems for each.

> AGENT

I couldn’t read `~/Documents/ARnold` directly because macOS blocks this process from that folder, but I found the Arnold spec in the Megaplan clone at `planning-bot-spec.md`. That’s enough to reason about the model.

I think the right synthesis is:

**Epic Is The Product/Planning Object**
An Epic is the long-lived thinking artifact:
- goal
- body / design doc
- decisions
- open questions
- checklist
- code references
- images / research
- sprint breakdown
- history

Arnold’s definition is strong: an epic becomes `planned` when the body is handoff-ready and every sprint is either `queued` or `pending`.

**RunGraph Is The Execution Object**
A RunGraph is what happens when queued work starts:
- items
- dependencies
- attempts
- worktrees
- cloud/local backend
- merge policy
- PRs
- logs
- status
- retries

So I would not collapse Epic and RunGraph into one thing. I’d link them.

The clean model is:

```text
Epic
  -> Sprints
      -> RunGraph Items
          -> Attempts
              -> Megaplan Plans
              -> Artifacts / Branches / PRs
```

In other words:

```text
Epic = why + what + shape
RunGraph = how + when + where + merge
Plan = one item’s reasoning/execution lifecycle
```

That gives you the best of both systems.

**How Arnold Folds Into Megaplan**

Arnold should probably produce the Epic and sprint breakdown. Megaplan should execute the queued sprint graph.

Example:

```yaml
epic:
  id: video-editor-dx
  title: Video Editor Developer Experience
  state: planned

sprints:
  - id: canonical-data-model
    status: queued
    queue_position: 1

  - id: ports-adapters
    status: queued
    queue_position: 2
    depends_on: [canonical-data-model]

  - id: docs-demo
    status: pending
    pending_reason: "Wait until core APIs settle"
```

Then Megaplan compiles queued sprints into:

```yaml
run_graph:
  id: video-editor-dx-execution
  source_epic: video-editor-dx

items:
  - id: canonical-data-model
    kind: megaplan
    idea: epic.sprints.canonical-data-model
    depends_on: []
    isolation: worktree
    merge: pr

  - id: ports-adapters
    kind: megaplan
    idea: epic.sprints.ports-adapters
    depends_on:
      - item: canonical-data-model
        requires: merged
```

**The Key Abstraction**

I’d make **Epic** the durable top-level planning abstraction, and **RunGraph** the durable top-level execution abstraction.

Do not make a chain the top-level thing. Do not make a plan the top-level thing.

Use this hierarchy:

```text
Epic
  body: markdown planning artifact
  checklist: planning process
  sprints: proposed / queued / pending
  state: shaping / sprinting / planned / paused / archived

RunGraph
  source_epic_id
  items: executable units
  dependencies
  scheduler
  backend
  merge policy

Plan
  belongs_to: run_graph_item_attempt
  handles: prep / plan / critique / gate / execute / review
```

**Why This Is Better**

Arnold already has concepts Megaplan is missing:
- planned vs not planned
- queued vs pending
- queue positions
- pending reasons
- force-through with audit
- section-addressed living docs
- epic history / events
- handoff readiness gates

Megaplan already has concepts Arnold should not own:
- execution phases
- worker orchestration
- cloud worker
- worktrees
- retries
- PR/merge path
- CI/test verification
- agent execution artifacts

So the clean architecture is not “merge Arnold into Megaplan” or “Megaplan replaces Arnold.” It’s:

**Arnold shapes epics. Megaplan executes run graphs generated from epics.**

**First-Class Commands**

Eventually I’d want:

```bash
megaplan epic import arnold <epic-id>
megaplan epic show video-editor-dx
megaplan run create --from-epic video-editor-dx --queued-only
megaplan run start video-editor-dx --cloud
megaplan run status video-editor-dx
```

And from Arnold:

```text
"Start the queued sprints for this epic in Megaplan"
```

That creates or updates the RunGraph, then Megaplan owns execution.

The main design decision: **Epic and RunGraph should be separate but linked.** That’s the abstraction that keeps long-term product thinking and concrete agent execution from getting tangled.

> DEVELOPER

And I guess it should proceed through plans based on dependencies, but also across different projects. So it should just be running all the open plans that don't have dependencies that are in a certain state. Is that correct? And they can either be inserted directly or via epics. And those epics can either be created directly or via the Arnold process. Can you just think that through? Just to think through how the data meets.

> AGENT

Yes, that is the right direction.

The core abstraction should be a **global work queue of executable items**, not a single chain tied to one repo. Chains, epics, and direct plans are all just different ways of creating queue items.

**The Data Model**

Think of it as four layers:

```text
Epic
  -> Work Items
      -> Attempts
          -> Plans
```

Or more broadly:

```text
Source Artifact -> Work Item -> Attempt -> Execution Artifact
```

Where source artifact can be:
- an Arnold-created epic
- a manually created epic
- a direct user request
- an imported GitHub issue
- a generated follow-up from another plan
- a scheduled maintenance task

**Epic**
An epic is the shaped product/planning object.

```ts
Epic {
  id
  title
  body
  state: "shaping" | "sprinting" | "planned" | "paused" | "archived"
  source: "arnold" | "manual" | "imported"
}
```

**Work Item**
A work item is the executable unit. This is the important queue primitive.

```ts
WorkItem {
  id
  title
  source_type: "epic_sprint" | "direct_plan" | "github_issue" | "generated_followup"
  source_id
  project_id
  repo
  status: "blocked" | "ready" | "running" | "done_unmerged" | "merged" | "failed" | "cancelled"
  priority
  depends_on[]
  required_dependency_state: "done" | "approved" | "merged" | "pr_opened"
  isolation: "same_checkout" | "worktree" | "cloud_workspace"
  merge_policy: "none" | "patch" | "pr" | "stacked_pr" | "auto_merge"
}
```

**Attempt**
An attempt is one concrete try at doing a work item.

```ts
Attempt {
  id
  work_item_id
  attempt_number
  backend: "local" | "cloud"
  worker
  started_at
  ended_at
  status
  branch
  worktree_path
  plan_id
}
```

**Plan**
A plan is still Megaplan’s phase lifecycle: prep, plan, critique, gate, execute, review.

```ts
Plan {
  id
  attempt_id
  state
  artifacts
}
```

**Scheduler Rule**

The scheduler should continuously ask:

> Which work items are open, unblocked, allowed to run, and not conflicting with currently running items?

An item is runnable when:
- its status is `ready` or `blocked` but now unblocked
- all dependencies are in the required state
- its project is not paused
- it has not exceeded retry policy
- its write scope does not conflict with a running item, unless isolation allows it
- worker capacity exists
- required secrets/backend are available

So yes, it should run all open plans/items whose dependencies are satisfied and whose scheduling constraints allow them.

**Across Projects**

Projects should be first-class grouping, but not hard execution boundaries.

```ts
Project {
  id
  name
  repos[]
  default_backend
  default_merge_policy
  paused
}
```

The global scheduler can run work across projects:

```text
Project A: ready item 1
Project B: ready item 2
Project C: blocked on item 9
```

If capacity is 3, it can run ready items from different projects at the same time.

**How Items Enter The Queue**

There are multiple ingestion paths:

```text
Arnold conversation
  -> Epic
      -> queued sprints
          -> WorkItems

Manual epic
  -> queued sprints
      -> WorkItems

Direct Megaplan request
  -> WorkItem directly
      -> Plan attempt

GitHub issue
  -> WorkItem directly, or Epic if it needs shaping
```

So the system should support both:

```bash
megaplan work add "Fix timeline duration validation"
```

and:

```bash
megaplan work create-from-epic video-editor-dx --queued-only
```

**Important Distinction**

A queued sprint is not itself execution. It is a planning commitment.

A work item is execution-ready.

So Arnold might say:

```text
Sprint 2: queued
```

Megaplan then materializes:

```text
WorkItem: sprint-02-ports-adapters
status: ready
depends_on: sprint-01-canonical-data-model requires merged
```

**Dependency Semantics**

Dependencies should point to work items, not plans.

Bad:

```text
Plan B depends on Plan A
```

Better:

```text
WorkItem B depends on WorkItem A being merged
```

Because Plan A may fail and retry with Plan A2. The dependency should survive attempts.

**The Elegant Mental Model**

I’d phrase the whole system like this:

```text
Epics shape work.
Work items queue work.
Attempts execute work.
Plans reason through attempts.
Artifacts prove and merge work.
```

That keeps the layers clean.

**Recommended Product Shape**

Commands could become:

```bash
megaplan epic create ...
megaplan epic import arnold ...
megaplan work add ...
megaplan work from-epic <epic-id>
megaplan queue status
megaplan queue run --cloud
megaplan queue pause --project reigh
megaplan queue logs <work-item-id>
```

And status should be global:

```text
Queue
Running: 3
Ready: 5
Blocked: 12
Failed: 1

Running
- reigh/video-editor sprint-01 critique via codex
- megaplan/cloud doctor execute via codex
- arnold/supabase-adapter plan via codex

Ready
- reigh/video-editor sprint-02 blocked? no, ready after sprint-01 merged
- megaplan/docs cloud-readme
```

So yes: the system should not “run a chain.” It should maintain a durable queue of work items across projects, continuously starting anything whose dependencies and constraints are satisfied. Chains and epics become structured ways to populate that queue.

> AGENT

Execution is moving again, but it hit an infrastructure snag first.

Current state:
- Chain is on Sprint 1: `sprint-01-canonical-data-model`.
- Plan created: `video-editor-developer-20260502-1729`.
- Planning phase completed successfully: `plan_v1.md` exists.
- It is now running `critique via codex`.
- The active critique process is healthy: ~2m13s elapsed at last poll, expected window up to 15m.
- The active Codex command is now using `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox`, which is the correct mode for Railway.

What went wrong:
- Earlier critique attempts failed because Codex tried to use bubblewrap sandboxing inside Railway.
- Railway denies the namespace creation, so every filesystem operation failed with `bwrap: Creating new namespace failed: Permission denied`.
- After five failed critique attempts, the chain marked the milestone as `stalled`.

What I fixed:
- Patched Megaplan so `MEGAPLAN_TRUSTED_CONTAINER=1` bypasses Codex sandboxing for all phases, not just execute.
- Patched the cloud entrypoint to export trusted-container mode.
- Patched `cloud chain` so future chain runs start with `MEGAPLAN_TRUSTED_CONTAINER=1`.
- Installed the fix on the worker and restarted the chain.

So, short version: Sprint 1 is in progress again. It is not yet through critique/gate/execute, but the blocker was diagnosed and fixed, and the current critique is running with the correct cloud-safe Codex flags.

> AGENT

I checked the actual code, and my read is mixed:

**Sprint 1 quality: decent architecture, too much blast radius.** The core idea is good: `src/tools/video-editor/domain/timeline/` creates a real canonical boundary with separate config-only and registry/document-aware normalization. That is the right shape for making later ports/adapters and command work cleaner.

The good parts:
- The domain split is coherent: `config.ts`, `document.ts`, `duration.ts`, `issues.ts`, `shared.ts`.
- Editor rebuild paths now flow through `buildTimelineData()` / `normalizeTimelineDocument()`.
- Backend load/save now uses shared canonical hooks in `ai-timeline-agent/db.ts`.
- Duration calculations were pulled into shared helpers instead of being reimplemented in UI/backend spots.
- Targeted tests passed in the receipts: typecheck, focused frontend suites, focused edge suites.

The concerning parts:
- The patch is too broad for Sprint 1: roughly 2.7k changed lines across ~40 files. That is not elegant yet; it’s a fairly invasive migration.
- Validation is weaker than the naming suggests. `validateTimelineConfig()` mainly checks unexpected keys. Domain normalization can produce issue objects with `severity: "error"` for invalid trim ranges, but several serialization/validation paths only call `assertValidTimelineConfig()`, which only reruns unexpected-key validation. So “structured issues” exist, but they are not consistently enforced.
- Some legacy migration behavior appears copied into the new domain `shared.ts` instead of clearly retired or wrapped. That is pragmatic, but it means the new “canonical model” still carries legacy-specific assumptions.
- The current cloud worktree is now polluted by Sprint 2 in-progress changes, so the current diff is no longer a clean Sprint 1 review surface.
- Sprint 2 execution has already hit at least one timeout and resumed; that’s a reliability/DX signal even if the process is continuing.

My judgement: **Sprint 1 is probably mergeable only after a human pass and cleanup, not “ship it untouched.”** The architecture direction is good. The execution is competent, but agent-y: broad edits, some overclaiming in artifacts, and a few semantic gaps where the new API’s names imply stronger guarantees than the implementation currently provides.

I’d harden before merging by doing three things:
1. Make domain errors actually block in document/save paths, or rename the validators so they don’t imply full semantic validation.
2. Separate Sprint 1 diff from Sprint 2 changes so review has a clean boundary.
3. Remove or quarantine `.megaplan` schema/chain artifacts and any unrelated generated changes from the app patch.

> AGENT

Because Sprint 2’s own reviewer found **real rework**, not just a flaky status.

Main reasons it’s not clean:

1. **Command core duplication**
   The sprint wanted one shared pure command core used by frontend and backend. It created `src/tools/video-editor/commands/core.ts`, but the mounted frontend facade in `src/tools/video-editor/commands/index.ts` reimplements the same command bodies instead of wrapping the core.

2. **The duplicated implementations already diverged**
   Reviewer found a concrete bug: `core.ts` guards `nextState.registry.assets ??= {}`, but `commands/index.ts` writes into `nextState.registry.assets[input.assetId]` without ensuring `assets` exists. That’s exactly the kind of drift the sprint was supposed to prevent.

3. **Broad verification is still red**
   Targeted video-editor/backend tests passed, but broad tests still failed. Some may be unrelated or flaky, but the sprint success criteria said broader commands should complete successfully. The latest full frontend run failed one test timeout:
   `GenerationHelpPopover.test.tsx`.

4. **The diff is huge**
   Reviewer flagged ~8,414 changed lines across 87 files. For a “ports/adapters + command facade” sprint, that’s a lot of surface area and makes accidental behavior change more likely.

5. **Megaplan is currently re-executing to repair**
   Status shows all tasks were completed once, review existed, then `execute` had an `error`, and a new execute phase started. That means the system itself is trying to fix rework.

So “not clean” means: **the work is progressing and has useful structure, but Sprint 2 has at least one architectural violation plus a concrete implementation bug, and review has not passed yet.**

```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: docs/quality/video_editor_megaplan_sprint_review_2026-05-02.md\n+# Video Editor Megaplan Sprint Review\n+\n+Date: 2026-05-02\n+\n+Scope:\n+- Sprint 1 plan: `video-editor-developer-20260502-1729`\n+- Sprint 2 plan: `video-editor-developer-20260502-1920`\n+- Cloud chain: `.megaplan/video-editor-dx-chain.yaml`\n+\n+This is a shared review document for issues to investigate or fix before treating the cloud Megaplan output as merge-ready. It separates application code quality from Megaplan/cloud workflow quality.\n+\n+## Current State\n+\n+Sprint 1 completed through review and was marked `approved`.\n+\n+Sprint 2 is not clean yet. It completed all tracked tasks once, produced a `review.json`, and that review returned `needs_rework`. Megaplan then re-entered execute to repair the findings. At the last status check, Sprint 2 was still in `execute`, with 7/7 tasks and 6/6 batches recorded as complete, but the last completed execute attempt had `result: error`.\n+\n+## Must Fix Before Merge\n+\n+### 1. Sprint 2 has two command implementations\n+\n+Severity: high\n+\n+Files:\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/commands/core.ts`\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/commands/index.ts`\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/hooks/useTimelineState.ts`\n+- `supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tools/timeline.ts`\n+\n+Problem:\n+Sprint 2 was supposed to create one shared pure command core with thin frontend/backend adapters. The backend wrappers use `commands/core.ts`, but the mounted frontend facade imports `createTimelineCommandFacade` from `commands/index.ts`, which reimplements the same command bodies instead of wrapping the core.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+This leaves two mutation engines for the same public operations. The review already found real drift: `core.ts` initializes `nextState.registry.assets ??= {}` in `registerAsset()`, while `index.ts` writes to `nextState.registry.assets[input.assetId]` without guaranteeing `assets` exists.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Make `commands/index.ts` a mounted adapter over `commands/core.ts`. It should handle editor-specific concerns only: getting current state, applying the returned state through `commitData`, selection side effects, semantic labels, and conversion to mounted return types. The edit semantics should live only in `core.ts`.\n+\n+### 2. Sprint 2 broad verification is still red\n+\n+Severity: high\n+\n+Files/tests reported by Megaplan review:\n+- `supabase/functions/ai-generate-effect/index.test.ts`\n+- `supabase/functions/ai-voice-prompt/index.test.ts`\n+- `supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.test.ts`\n+- `src/shared/components/ui/overlay/dropdown-menu.test.tsx`\n+- later full frontend run also timed out in `src/shared/components/SettingsModal/sections/GenerationSection/components/GenerationHelpPopover.test.tsx`\n+\n+Problem:\n+Targeted video-editor and backend suites passed, but broad test commands did not complete green. Some failures may be unrelated or flaky, but Sprint 2's success criteria required broad verification to pass.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+The Sprint 2 diff touches runtime boundaries, shared contexts, edge functions, and test files outside the video editor. Until the broad failures are explained or fixed, it is not clear whether the refactor caused collateral damage.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Classify each failing broad test as one of:\n+- caused by the Megaplan changes and fixed in this branch;\n+- pre-existing and documented with evidence from main;\n+- flaky and rerun green with a note.\n+\n+Do not merge with only \"targeted tests pass\" as the evidence.\n+\n+### 3. Sprint 2 patch is too broad for confident review\n+\n+Severity: high\n+\n+Evidence:\n+Sprint 2 review flagged roughly 8,414 changed lines across 87 files. Current cloud `git diff --stat` later showed 76 files changed with 1,650 insertions and 1,681 deletions, but the worktree had already been modified by repair attempts.\n+\n+Problem:\n+The diff spans ports/adapters, command facade, UI components, shared `AgentChatContext`, edge functions, tests, and generated Megaplan/schema files.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+This makes review hard and increases regression risk. It also means the cloud worktree is no longer a clean per-sprint review surface.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Split into reviewable patches:\n+1. Runtime/host port definitions and Reigh adapters.\n+2. Shared command core and frontend mounted adapter.\n+3. Backend wrapper migration to shared command core.\n+4. Tests and docs.\n+\n+Generated Megaplan/schema/chain files should not be part of the app patch.\n+\n+## Sprint 1 Issues To Investigate\n+\n+### 4. Sprint 1 validation names overstate semantic guarantees\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Files:\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/domain/timeline/config.ts`\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/domain/timeline/document.ts`\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/lib/serialize.ts`\n+\n+Problem:\n+Sprint 1 added structured domain issues and helpers such as `validateTimelineConfig()` and `assertValidTimelineConfig()`. The implementation mostly checks unexpected keys. Semantic issues produced during normalization, such as invalid trim ranges, are returned as issue objects but are not consistently used as blocking validation in serialize/save paths.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+A developer reading the API may assume `validateTimelineConfig()` fully validates the timeline domain. It does not. That creates a misleading developer experience and can let semantically invalid timelines pass unless callers manually inspect normalization issues.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Either:\n+- make document/save paths fail on `hasTimelineDomainErrors(normalized.issues)`, or\n+- rename/split the APIs so structural key validation and semantic normalization issues are obviously separate.\n+\n+Recommended public shape:\n+- `validateTimelineShape(config)`\n+- `normalizeTimelineConfig(config): { config, issues }`\n+- `assertNoTimelineDomainErrors(issues)`\n+- `normalizeTimelineDocument(..., { throwOnError?: true })`\n+\n+### 5. Sprint 1 canonical domain still carries legacy migration behavior\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Files:\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/domain/timeline/shared.ts`\n+- `src/tools/video-editor/domain/timeline/config.ts`\n+\n+Problem:\n+The new canonical domain boundary includes legacy track mapping and legacy effect migration behavior. That may be necessary for compatibility, but it makes the domain layer less clean than the name suggests.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+Future developers may treat the domain module as a small canonical model when it also performs historical migrations and product-specific repairs. That is workable, but it needs to be explicit.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Move legacy migration policy into clearly named helpers inside the domain package, for example:\n+- `legacy.ts`\n+- `normalizeLegacyTimelineShape()`\n+- `repairPinnedShotGroups()`\n+\n+Then make `normalizeTimelineConfig()` read as orchestration over named phases.\n+\n+### 6. Sprint 1 had no automated editor smoke proof\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Files/artifacts:\n+- `.megaplan/plans/video-editor-developer-20260502-1729/review.json`\n+\n+Problem:\n+Sprint 1 review approved the code but waived manual editor smoke coverage. The code touches render duration, preview, provider/load-save, poll sync, backend placement, and pinned shot groups.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+The change may be correct at the data layer but still regress mounted editor behavior. Automated unit tests do not prove basic editor interactions still work.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Add a small smoke checklist or Playwright flow before merge:\n+- load an existing timeline;\n+- add a clip;\n+- trim/move it;\n+- verify preview duration remains sensible;\n+- save/reload;\n+- verify no poll-sync conflict loop;\n+- verify pinned shot group contiguity does not corrupt the timeline.\n+\n+### 7. Sprint 1 artifacts overclaimed one documentation change\n+\n+Severity: low\n+\n+File:\n+- `docs/video_editor_canonical_timeline_sprint1.md`\n+\n+Problem:\n+Sprint 1 claimed this doc was changed, but review/audit said it was already present and not in the current diff.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+This is not a code bug, but it reduces confidence in execution receipts.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Treat Megaplan file-claim mismatches as review warnings. Require the final review summary to distinguish \"content exists\" from \"this plan changed it.\"\n+\n+## Megaplan / Cloud Workflow Issues\n+\n+### 8. Chained sprints are sharing one dirty cloud worktree\n+\n+Severity: high\n+\n+Problem:\n+Sprint 2 started in the same cloud worktree after Sprint 1. The current diff now contains Sprint 1 and Sprint 2 changes together, plus repair attempts. That makes it hard to review, revert, or merge one sprint independently.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+For this style of chained work, each sprint needs an isolation boundary. Otherwise \"Sprint 2 quality\" and \"Sprint 1 quality\" become entangled.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Make chain execution first-class around work items and attempts:\n+- each sprint runs in its own branch/worktree;\n+- successful sprint output is reviewed and merged into a known integration branch;\n+- the next sprint starts from that integration branch;\n+- generated Megaplan state stays outside the app diff or is ignored.\n+\n+### 9. Chain state/status can be misleading after retries\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Examples observed:\n+- `chain_state.json` initially retained `last_state: \"stalled\"` after a manual restart while the plan was actually healthy.\n+- Sprint 2 status showed all tasks complete while active step remained `execute`.\n+- `last_step` can show an earlier timeout/error while a repair execute is currently healthy.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+Humans need to know whether a plan is done, repairing, stalled, or safe to review. Current status requires interpretation across `state`, `active_step`, `last_step`, `progress`, and `chain_state`.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Add explicit high-level statuses:\n+- `running`\n+- `repairing`\n+- `waiting_review`\n+- `done`\n+- `failed`\n+- `stalled`\n+\n+When a repair starts, show: \"repairing after review failure\" or \"retrying after execute timeout\" instead of leaving that inference to the operator.\n+\n+### 10. Execution progress is too coarse inside batches\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Problem:\n+During execution, status only updates between batches. Long batch runs show a live Codex process but not what it is doing.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+This makes it hard to distinguish productive work from a stuck agent until timeout.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Write lightweight heartbeat/progress events from execute:\n+- current batch number;\n+- current task id;\n+- last command started/finished;\n+- current file focus if available;\n+- last artifact write time.\n+\n+The `execution_trace.jsonl` should be surfaced by `megaplan status`.\n+\n+### 11. Railway API/SSH timeouts interrupt observability\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Examples observed:\n+- Railway GraphQL request timeout during status polling.\n+- Railway WebSocket timeout while reading artifacts.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+The plan can be healthy while the operator sees provider errors. That invites unnecessary restarts.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Add retry/backoff to `megaplan cloud exec/status`, and prefer reading cached status artifacts from the worker when possible. Provider failures should be reported as \"observer failed, plan state unknown\" rather than plan failure.\n+\n+### 12. Generated Megaplan files appear in the app diff\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Files repeatedly seen in cloud `git status`:\n+- `.megaplan/debt.json`\n+- `.megaplan/schemas/*.json`\n+- `chain.yaml`\n+- `chain_state.json`\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+These files pollute code review and confuse execution audits as \"unclaimed files.\"\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+Separate Megaplan runtime state from repo changes:\n+- write generated schemas/state under an ignored runtime directory;\n+- or add exact ignore rules;\n+- or commit intentional project config separately from generated run state.\n+\n+### 13. Timeout/retry semantics need clearer finalization\n+\n+Severity: medium\n+\n+Problem:\n+Sprint 2 had an execute timeout, later review returned `needs_rework`, then a repair execute started. Status exposed these facts, but the lifecycle was not obvious.\n+\n+Why this matters:\n+An operator needs to know whether the plan is still attempting repair, whether the review failed, and whether it will automatically review again after repair.\n+\n+Fix direction:\n+After `review: needs_rework`, Megaplan should create an explicit repair attempt:\n+- `attempt: 2`\n+- `reason: review_failed`\n+- `blocking_findings: [...]`\n+- next expected phase after execute: `review`\n+\n+## Merge Recommendation\n+\n+Do not merge the combined cloud worktree as-is.\n+\n+Recommended path:\n+1. Let Sprint 2 finish its repair and review loop.\n+2. Export clean per-sprint diffs or branches.\n+3. Review and land Sprint 1 separately after addressing or explicitly accepting its medium-risk items.\n+4. For Sprint 2, require the command facade duplication to be fixed before merge.\n+5. Keep Megaplan runtime artifacts out of the app patch.\n+\n+## Quick Triage Table\n+\n+| Area | Status | Merge stance |\n+| --- | --- | --- |\n+| Sprint 1 canonical domain | Approved with caveats | Reviewable after smoke/validation cleanup decision |\n+| Sprint 2 runtime ports/adapters | Mostly landed | Needs final review after repair |\n+| Sprint 2 command facade | Failing reviewer must criterion | Must fix |\n+| Broad tests | Red or not classified | Must classify/fix |\n+| Cloud chain mechanics | Working but noisy | Improve status/attempt/worktree model |\n+| Combined cloud diff | Dirty and cross-sprint | Do not merge as one patch |\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```
